Report Issue Back to Abstract Download PDF Abstract 1 Introduction 2 Programming language for complexity analysis 3 Meaning explanation 4 Constructing a type system 5 Computational Type Theory 6 Semantic Proof Theory 6.1 Universes 6.2 Equality 6.3 Nat 6.4 Subset 6.5 Relations 6.6 Dependent Product 6.7 Funtime 6.8 Weakening 7 Machines 8 Example: greatest common divisor 9 Parallelism 10 Example: Fibonacci number 11 Related work 12 Conclusion & future work References License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2011.03660v2 [cs.PL] 10 Nov 2020 Cost-Aware Type Theory Yue Niu Note: yuen@andrew.cmu.edu Affiliation: Carnegie Mellon University Robert Harper Note: rwh@cs.cmu.edu Affiliation: Carnegie Mellon University Abstract Although computational complexity is a fundamental aspect of program behavior, it is often at odds with common type theoretic principles such as function extensionality, which identifies all functions with the same input-output behavior. We present a computational type theory called 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} that has a primitive notion of cost (the number of evaluation steps). We introduce a new dependent function type “funtime” whose semantics can be viewed as a cost-aware version of function extensionality. We prove a collection of lemmas for 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} , including a novel introduction rule for the new funtime type. 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} can be simultaneously viewed as a framework for analyzing computational complexity of programs and as the beginnings of a semantic foundation for characterizing feasible mathematical proofs. 1 Introduction Beginning with mechanized time complexity analysis of Lisp programs [ 68 ] , computational complexity in the context of programming language research has been studied from various perspectives, including definability of complexity classes inside type theory [ 21 ] [ 22 ] , syntactic characterizations of complexity classes [ 7 ] [ 41 ] [ 40 ] , recurrence extraction [ 9 ] [ 48 ] , substructural & resource aware type systems [ 42 ] [ 45 ] [ 36 ] , and program logics for interactive verification of cost bounds [ 19 ] [ 54 ] [ 32 ] . What seemed to be missing from the discussion was a semantic theory with a rich specification language in which computational complexity is a primitive notion. This is the main motivation behind the work in this article. The shift in perspective is subtle: instead of analyzing programs after the fact, we develop a theory for simultaneous construction of cost-aware specification and programs satisfying those specifications. This perspective on computational complexity resonates with a distinctive feature in dependent type theory: specifications are programs, and the construction of one is inextricably intertwined with the other. We develop a computational dependent type theory, called 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} (cost-aware type theory), for analyzing the time complexity of programs. The central move borrows from the Martin-Löf ethos in the formulation of computational type theories: type structure internalizes prior judgmental notions. Following this idea, we introduce new judgment forms to describe computational complexity. A new dependent function type “funtime” is introduced to internalize the meaning of open judgments. We prove a novel introduction rule for the funtime type. The proof relies on the fact that a terminating recursive function induces a well-founded ordering on the recursive calls (a similar idea was introduced in [ 57 ] to add general recursion to Martin-Löf type theories). A perhaps surprising fact is that the judgments in 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} only speak about total terms: there is no judgment form that acknowledges partiality. However, this is not achieved through syntactic means (such as Gödel’s T or system F). While syntactic restrictions alleviate the burden of the termination proof from the user, total languages are susceptible to arbitrary blow up in code size when compared to languages with general recursion [ 14 ] . 1 1 1 An article by Robert Harper discusses this in detail: https://existentialtype.wordpress.com/2014/03/20/old-neglected-theorems-are-still-theorems/. Practically, programs in total languages can also have unexpected computational complexity. For example, a more liberal form of recursion can be simulated in Gödel’s T by proving course-of-value (COV) recursion (the dependently typed version is strong induction). One can then program via the COV-recursion interface, but the resulting algorithm will not have the expected recursive behavior: every recursive argument is considered, regardless of whether it was requested by the algorithm. This seems to be a fundamental limitation of programming with primitive recursion. Hence, while programs in Gödel’s T can be written in a more natural manner using an interface such as COV-recursion, their computational behavior and as a result complexity does not coincide with textbook algorithm analysis. The key innovation in 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} leverages the synergetic relation between general recursion and cost specification: general recursion is needed to write algorithms with the expected cost, and the cost specification in turn enables one to prove that a general recursive function is total (and satisfies its functional specification). This enables the programmer to write general recursive functions that are not a priori terminating and simultaneously construct the proof that it is in fact terminating using the cost specification. As a consequence, the membership judgment M ∈ A M\in A immediately implies M M terminates, and all well-typed functions are total (an immediate consequence of adhering to a cost specification). Language vs. machine Before diving into the specifics of 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} , we take a moment to frame our work in the bigger landscape. While the programming languages theory encompasses nearly all imaginable computational behavior – e.g., state, randomization, parallelism, concurrency – language-based semantics for computational complexity has received comparatively little attention. Instead, machine models such as the Turing machine and RAM are incumbent in algorithmic complexity literature. Part of the reason for this discrepancy could be attributed to the belief that machine models are “lower-level” and so the induced computational complexity is a more accurate description of program performance in real life. However, it should be noted that computational complexity defined in a language-based model (such as 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} ) do reflect real-life performance, as long as the implementation is resource bounded [ 12 ] [ 31 ] [ 13 ] [ 11 ] [ 61 ] by the cost semantics. This means that each stage of compilation (from the source language to the target language) is cost-preserving: the cost semantics of the higher-level language induces an upper bound on the cost semantics of the lower-level language. This process separates the concern of the algorithm analysis from low-level, implementation specific concerns that are asymptotically irrelevant in any case. Furthermore, language-based models have some clear advantages compared to machine-models such as compositionality and modularity. Hence, a possible direction for future work would be designing intermediate languages for complexity analysis and the associated cost-preserving compilations, which would lead to a comprehensive framework for describing both high-level and low-level computational complexity and the effects of different compilation/scheduling strategies. Outline In the following sections, we motivate and develop 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} and demonstrate the applicability of the theory with examples. In section 2 , we set the stage by defining the underlying programming language 𝐏𝐋 \mathbf{PL} and discuss some considerations when choosing an appropriate language for analyzing computational complexity. In section 3 , we give some intuition for 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} by extending Martin-Löf’s meaning explanation for type theories to account for computational complexity. In section 4 , we realize 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} via Allen’s PER-semantics [ 3 ] for computational type theories, which involves constructing a relation τ : 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝖵𝖺𝗅 × 𝖵𝖺𝗅 × 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝖵𝖺𝗅 × 𝖵𝖺𝗅 ) ) \tau:\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Val}\times\mathsf{Val}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Val}\times\mathsf{Val})) satisfying some coherence conditions. A coherent relation τ \tau is then a type system (in a technical sense), and the judgments of 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} are defined relative to a given type system τ \tau . In section 5 , we define the judgments of 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} and prove some expected lemmas, including head expansion and the structural rules of hypothesis, weakening, and substitution. In section 6 , we prove a collection of lemmas about the semantics of 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} constituting a simple proof theory that we later use to verify some example programs. In section 7 , we discuss the usual assumptions made in algorithmic complexity and how they can be dealt with inside type theory. In section 8 , we analyze the time complexity of Euclid’s algorithm for computing the greatest common divisor. In section 9 and 10 , we extend 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} to account for parallelism and illustrate the extension by verifying the parallel complexity of computing the Fibonacci numbers. In section 11 and 12 , we discuss some related work and promising directions for investigation. 2 Programming language for complexity analysis 𝖤𝗑𝗉 E \displaystyle\mathsf{Exp}\quad E : := a | a : E 1 → E 2 ​ [ E 3 ] | a : E 1 → E 2 ​ ∣ 𝚏𝚞𝚗 ​ f ​ a ​ 𝚒𝚜 ​ E ​ ∣ E 1 ​ E 2 ∣ ​ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ∣ ​ 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 | 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( E ) \displaystyle::=a\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a{:}E_{1}\to E_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{[E_{3}]}}\mid a{:}E_{1}\to E_{2}\mid\mathtt{fun}\,f\,a\,\mathtt{is}\,E\mid E_{1}\;E_{2}\mid\mathtt{nat}\mid{\mathtt{zero}}\mid{\mathtt{suc}}(E) ∣ 𝚒𝚏𝚣 ( E ) { E 0 ; a . Although there are various abstract notions of computational complexity, we focus on analyzing a simple and concrete cost measure – the number of steps a closed program takes to evaluate to a value. Under this view of complexity, the analysis is fundamentally about the specification and verification of behavioral properties of programs. Hence, the very nature of computational complexity suggests itself as an object of study inside computational type theories , in which typehood and membership are semantic properties, i.e., given by how programs compute. In the influential paper Constructive Mathematics and Computer Programming [ 53 ] , Martin-Löf introduced a computational semantics for programming languages through what is known as the “meaning explanation”, a notion closely related to Tait’s method of logical relations [ 62 ] and Girard’s method of candidates of reducibility [ 30 ] . For dependent type theory, the same perspectives can also be found in the computational type theory of NuPRL [ 2 ] , a program/proof refinement framework which has been used to verify a substantial amount of mathematics. Programming language 𝐏𝐋 \mathbf{PL} Following these computational type-theoretic approaches, we develop a dependent type theory called 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} (complexity-aware type theory) for analyzing computational complexity of programs. We begin by defining a programming language 𝐏𝐋 \mathbf{PL} and an evaluation relation. The syntax of 𝐏𝐋 \mathbf{PL} is given in Figure 1 . Note that since typehood is a semantic property, there is no syntactic distinction between types and elements of a type. The value relation _ ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 : 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝖤𝗑𝗉 ) \_\;\mathsf{val}:\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Exp}) and step relation _ ↦ _ : 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝖤𝗑𝗉 × 𝖤𝗑𝗉 ) \_\mapsto\_:\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Exp}\times\mathsf{Exp}) are defined in Figure 2 . Write 𝖵𝖺𝗅 = { E ∣ E ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 } \mathsf{Val}=\{E\mid E\;\mathsf{val}\} for the set of closed programs that are values. Define evaluation as follows: 1. M ⇓ c V M\Downarrow^{c}V when M ↦ c V M\mapsto^{c}V and V ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 V\;\mathsf{val} . 2. M ⇓ V M\Downarrow V when there is a c c s.t. M ⇓ c V M\Downarrow^{c}V . Where M ↦ c V M\mapsto^{c}V means M M transition to V V in exactly c c steps. Complexity-aware function types In anticipation of analyzing complexity properties of programs, we introduce the new funtime type a : A → B ⁡ [ P ] {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a{:}A\to B}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{[P]}} , which classify functions that comply with the cost bound stated by P P . Crucially, P P is an open term of 𝐏𝐋 \mathbf{PL} and not a natural number since the cost P P varies in the input value. Furthermore, it is also not a meta-theoretical function, because the variable a a ranges over terms of 𝐏𝐋 \mathbf{PL} . This means we cannot directly use the ambient arithmetic functions in defining the cost component P P . One route would be to define all the usual arithmetic functions internally. This turns out to burden the mechanization process with many secondary proof obligations which hold in the ambient mathematics. Instead, we import a part of the ambient arithmetic world as foreign functions . Foreign functions We introduce several syntactic operators that mediate the use of meta-theoretical functions or relations on natural numbers. The expression E 1 r ^ E 2 E_{1}\mathrel{\widehat{r}}E_{2} reifies a (meta-)relation r : ℕ × ℕ r:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N} into a type, and f 1 ^ ⁡ ( E ) \mathop{\hat{f_{1}}}(E) and ( E 1 f 2 ^ E 2 ) (E_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{f_{2}}}E_{2}) form applications of (meta-)functions f 1 : ℕ → ℕ f_{1}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N} and f 2 : ℕ → ℕ → ℕ f_{2}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N} , respectively. In principle, we can define these constructs internally. However, we found it more practical to import these arithmetical facts and use them directly in the verification. Evaluation strategy Usually, computational type theories are agnostic to the evaluation strategy employed by the programming language, i.e., one can define a type theory with either call by value or call by name evaluation. However, when considering computational complexity, different evaluation strategies can lead to wildly different evaluation costs. Often, call by name evaluation is favored because fewer rules are needed to specify the transition relation. This poses a barrier for complexity analysis, since if arbitrary computations can be applied to a function, it becomes difficult to prove uniform or general complexity properties about the function. Therefore, we opt for call by value evaluation; as a result, variables only range over values (elements of 𝖵𝖺𝗅 \mathsf{Val} ) in open term of 𝐏𝐋 \mathbf{PL} . a : E 1 → E 2 ​ [ E 3 ] ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 a : E 1 → E 2 ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 λ ​ x . Note that m ¯ = 𝚜𝚞𝚌 m ​ ( 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 ) \bar{m}=\mathtt{suc}^{m}({\mathtt{zero}}) . 3 Meaning explanation Given a programming language, the meaning explanation propounded by Martin-Löf [ 53 ] specifies the meaning of types and membership via the execution behavior of the underlying programs; we give a brief introduction here. At the outset, there is the notion of canonical types. A canonical type is defined by specifying its equal canonical elements. This specification of equality is dependent on the canonical type in question, but in general it must be an equivalence relation on all members. In its basic form, the meaning explanation consists of four judgments: A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 M ∈ A \displaystyle A\;\mathsf{type}\qquad M\in A A ≐ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 M ≐ M ′ ∈ A \displaystyle A\doteq A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type}\qquad M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A The first states that A A evaluates to a value A 0 A_{0} , and that A 0 A_{0} is a canonical type. Given that it is known that A A is a type (so that A A is known to evaluate to a canonical type A 0 A_{0} ), the second states that M M evaluates to M 0 M_{0} and M 0 M_{0} is a canonical element of A 0 A_{0} . The third states that A A and A ′ A^{\prime} evaluates to equal canonical types. Lastly, given that A A is a type, the fourth states that M M and M ′ M^{\prime} evaluates to equal canonical members of the canonical type that A A evaluates to. Following the philosophy that type constructors merely internalize prior judgmental structure, we add two new judgment forms, M ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} and M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} , which can be read as “ M M in A A with cost P P ” and “ M M equal to M ′ M^{\prime} in A A with cost P P ”. The first states that, presupposing A A is a type and P P is in 𝚗𝚊𝚝 \mathtt{nat} (by the way we specify 𝚗𝚊𝚝 \mathtt{nat} , this will mean P P evaluates to a numeral p ¯ \bar{p} ), M M evaluates to M 0 M_{0} in c c steps, M 0 M_{0} is in A A , and that p ≥ c p\geq c . Similarly, the second states that (with the same presuppositions) M M evaluates to M 0 M_{0} in c c steps, M ′ M^{\prime} evaluates to M 0 ′ M^{\prime}_{0} in d d steps, M 0 M_{0} and M 0 ′ M^{\prime}_{0} are equal in A A , and that p ≥ max ⁡ ( c , d ) p\geq\max{(c,d)} . These two judgments express the meaning of the previous membership and equality judgments and in addition prescribe the evaluation costs of the subjects. Here, the evaluation costs of A A and P P are not mentioned, since we are analyzing the computational complexity of running code and not its specifications. 2 2 2 If one desires, it is also possible to analyze the complexity of types by shifting the perspective and viewing them as elements of a universe. Note that a careful staging of definitions is needed to specify the cost-bounded judgmental forms. Prior to even stating M ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} , we need to know that P P is in 𝚗𝚊𝚝 \mathtt{nat} , and hence that 𝚗𝚊𝚝 \mathtt{nat} is a type. This also means it is not possible to define the judgment M ∈ A M\in A in terms of M ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} by existentially quantifying P P , which would lead to an infinite regress of presuppositions. 4 Constructing a type system The procedure given in the previous section of defining equal types and type membership is an instance of definition by induction-recursion, i.e., defining a structure inductively while simultaneously defining a function out of that structure. However, for mechanization purposes, it is more practical to follow the approach given by Allen [ 3 ] and define the judgments of type theory relative to a type system . Structurally, a type system is a relation τ : 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝖵𝖺𝗅 × 𝖵𝖺𝗅 × 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝖵𝖺𝗅 × 𝖵𝖺𝗅 ) ) \tau:\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Val}\times\mathsf{Val}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Val}\times\mathsf{Val})) . The idea is that A A and B B are equal types with membership relation ϕ \phi exactly when τ ⁡ ( A , B , ϕ ) \tau(A,B,\phi) . Observe that in the meaning explanation, 1) type equality and membership are partial equivalence relations, and 2) equal types classify the same terms. Hence, τ \tau must satisfy the same coherence properties in order to qualify as a type system, which can be summarized as follows. Definition 4.1 (Type Systems) . Write 𝖯𝖳𝖲 = 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝖵𝖺𝗅 × 𝖵𝖺𝗅 × 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝖵𝖺𝗅 × 𝖵𝖺𝗅 ) ) \mathsf{PTS}=\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Val}\times\mathsf{Val}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Val}\times\mathsf{Val})) for the space of all possible type systems . Let τ : 𝖯𝖳𝖲 \tau:\mathsf{PTS} . τ \tau is a type system when the following conditions hold: 1. Unicity: τ ⁡ ( A , B , ϕ ) \tau(A,B,\phi) and τ ⁡ ( A , B , ϕ ′ ) \tau(A,B,\phi^{\prime}) implies ϕ = ϕ ′ \phi=\phi^{\prime} . 2. PER valuation: τ ⁡ ( A , B , ϕ ) \tau(A,B,\phi) implies ϕ \phi is a PER. 3. Symmetry: τ ⁡ ( A , B , ϕ ) \tau(A,B,\phi) implies τ ⁡ ( B , A , ϕ ) \tau(B,A,\phi) . 4. Transitivity: τ ⁡ ( A , B , ϕ ) \tau(A,B,\phi) and τ ⁡ ( B , C , ϕ ) \tau(B,C,\phi) implies τ ⁡ ( A , C , ϕ ) \tau(A,C,\phi) . Now, to define a possible type system τ \tau closed under type constructors and to show that it is a type system, we follow the construction presented by Angiuli [ 4 ] and define two hierarchies of possible type systems τ n \tau_{n} and v n v_{n} , the latter of which forms a cumulative universe hierarchy. Roughly, each τ n \tau_{n} contains all types from the previous level and the universes in v n v_{n} and closes them under the type constructors, and v n v_{n} contains all universes 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i \mathtt{Univ}_{i} with i < n i<n . In Figure 3 , we define a function Types ​ ( v , τ ) \text{Types}(v,\tau) that closes τ \tau under type constructors and is monotone for fixed v v . For convenience, we introduce some notations that lift the type equality and membership relations along evaluation and to open terms. 1. A ∼ A ′ ↓ α ∈ τ A\sim A^{\prime}\downarrow\alpha\in\tau when A ⇓ A 0 A\Downarrow A_{0} , A ′ ⇓ A 0 ′ A^{\prime}\Downarrow A_{0}^{\prime} , and τ ⁡ ( A 0 , A 0 ′ , α ) \tau(A_{0},A_{0}^{\prime},\alpha) . 2. M ∼ M ′ ∈ α M\sim M^{\prime}\in\alpha when M ⇓ V M\Downarrow V , M ′ ⇓ V ′ M^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime} , and α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) . 3. a : α ​ ⊳ ​ B ∼ B ′ ↓ β ∈ τ a:\alpha\rhd B\sim B^{\prime}\downarrow\beta\in\tau when for all V , V ′ V,V^{\prime} s.t. α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) , [ V / a ] ​ B ∼ [ V ′ / a ] ​ B ′ ↓ β V , V ′ ∈ τ [V/a]B\sim[V^{\prime}/a]B^{\prime}\downarrow\beta_{V,V^{\prime}}\in\tau . 4. a : α ​ ⊳ ​ N ∼ N ′ ∈ β a{:}\alpha\rhd N\sim N^{\prime}\in\beta when for all V , V ′ V,V^{\prime} s.t. α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) , [ V / a ] ​ N ∼ [ V ′ / a ] ​ N ′ ∈ β V , V ′ [V/a]N\sim[V^{\prime}/a]N^{\prime}\in\beta_{V,V^{\prime}} . Note that these definitions are slightly different from those introduced in the “Idealized Nuprl” construction by Angiuli [ 4 ] : in the last two definitions, the open terms are only required to respect functionality for values , i.e. elements of 𝖵𝖺𝗅 \mathsf{Val} . As mentioned in Section 2 , this is because if arbitrary computations were also included, substitution instances of those open terms will not, in general, have uniform or predictable evaluation costs. Let X X be a complete lattice and f , g : X → X f,g:X\to X be monotone functions. If f ⁡ ( x ) ⊆ g ⁡ ( x ) f(x)\subseteq g(x) for all x ∈ X x\in X , then μ ​ x . f ⁡ ( x ) ⊆ μ ​ x . g ⁡ ( x ) \mu x.\,f(x)\subseteq\mu x.\,g(x) . It suffices to show Types ​ ( _ , τ ) \text{Types}(\_,\tau) is monotone, which holds by Lemma 4.2 . ∎ 5 Computational Type Theory Closed Judgments Having defined a type system τ ω \tau_{\omega} , we are now in a position to define the judgments of 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} . In addition to the usual type equality and membership relation, we define a third membership relation which bounds the evaluation cost. Definition 5.1 (Types and Equality) . Given a type system τ \tau , 1. A ≐ B ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 A\doteq B\;\mathsf{type} when A ∼ B ↓ α ∈ τ A\sim B\downarrow\alpha\in\tau for some α \alpha . 2. M ≐ M ′ ∈ A M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A when A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau and M ∼ M ′ ∈ α M\sim M^{\prime}\in\alpha for some α \alpha . 3. M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} when A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau and M ∼ M ′ ∈ α ⁡ [ P ] M\sim M^{\prime}\in\alpha\;{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{[P]}} for some α \alpha . 4. A ≐ B ​ 𝗍𝗋𝗎𝖾 A\doteq B\;\mathsf{true} when A ∼ B ↓ α ∈ τ A\sim B\downarrow\alpha\in\tau and M ∼ M ∈ α M\sim M\in\alpha for some α \alpha and M M . Remark . This definition is generic in a type system τ \tau . However, from now on we will implicitly assume τ = τ ω \tau=\tau_{\omega} unless specified otherwise. The reflexive case for the 3 relations will be abbreviated as follows: 1. A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 A\;\mathsf{type} means A ≐ A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 A\doteq A\;\mathsf{type} . 2. M ∈ A M\in A means M ≐ M ∈ A M\doteq M\in A . 3. M ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} means M ≐ M ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\doteq M\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} . Furthermore, when an expression is a value, membership will be subscripted with a 0: M ≐ M ′ ∈ 0 A M\doteq M^{\prime}\in_{0}A when M ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 M\;\mathsf{val} , M ′ ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 M^{\prime}\;\mathsf{val} , and M ≐ M ′ ∈ A M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A , and M ∈ 0 A M\in_{0}A when M ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 M\;\mathsf{val} and M ∈ A M\in A . The closed judgments are PERs: Lemma 5.1 (Closed PER) . 1. _ ≐ _ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \_\doteq\_\;\mathsf{type} is a PER. 2. _ ≐ _ ∈ A \_\doteq\_\in A is a PER. 3. _ ≐ _ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] \_\doteq\_\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} is a PER. Proof. Immediate consequence of the fact that τ ω \tau_{\omega} is a type system. ∎ Lemma 5.2 . Given A ≐ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 A\doteq A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} , 1. If M ≐ M ′ ∈ A M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A , then M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ′ M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A^{\prime} . 2. If M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} , then M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ′ ​ [ P ] M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} . Proof. Part 1) and 2) are similar, so we just show 1). Suppose M ≐ M ′ ∈ A M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A . This means A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} for some α \alpha and M ∼ M ′ ∈ α M\sim M^{\prime}\in\alpha . We need to show M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ′ M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A^{\prime} . By assumption, A ∼ A ′ ↓ α ′ ∈ τ ω A\sim A^{\prime}\downarrow\alpha^{\prime}\in\tau_{\omega} for some α ′ \alpha^{\prime} . By symmetry and transitivity, A ∼ A ↓ α ′ ∈ τ ω A\sim A\downarrow\alpha^{\prime}\in\tau_{\omega} , so by unicity, α = α ′ \alpha=\alpha^{\prime} . Similarly, A ′ ∼ A ′ ↓ α ′ ∈ τ ω A^{\prime}\sim A^{\prime}\downarrow\alpha^{\prime}\in\tau_{\omega} , and M ​ ⊳ ​ M ′ ∼ α ′ ∈ b M\rhd M^{\prime}\sim\alpha^{\prime}\in b y definition, so the result holds. ∎ Head expansion The closed judgments are closed under head expansion, and furthermore is a congruence with respect to complexity: Lemma 5.3 (Head expansion and replacement) . 1. If A ≐ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 A\doteq A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} and A ′′ ↦ A A^{\prime\prime}\mapsto A , then A ′′ ≐ A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 A^{\prime\prime}\doteq A\;\mathsf{type} . 2. If M ≐ M ′ ∈ A M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A and M ′′ ↦ M M^{\prime\prime}\mapsto M , then M ′′ ≐ M ′ ∈ A M^{\prime\prime}\doteq M^{\prime}\in A . 3. If M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} and M ′′ ↦ M M^{\prime\prime}\mapsto M , then M ′′ ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( P ) ] M^{\prime\prime}\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[{\mathtt{suc}}(P)]}} . 4. If M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} , M 1 ↦ c 1 M M_{1}\mapsto^{c_{1}}M , M 2 ↦ c 2 M ′ M_{2}\mapsto^{c_{2}}M^{\prime} , Q ⇓ q ¯ Q\Downarrow\bar{q} , and q ≥ max ⁡ ( c 1 , c 2 ) q\geq\max(c_{1},c_{2}) , then M 1 ≐ M 2 ∈ A ⁡ [ Q ​ + ^ ​ P ] M_{1}\doteq M_{2}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[Q\hat{+}P]}} . 5. If M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} and P ≐ P ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 P\doteq P^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat} , then M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ′ ] M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P^{\prime}]}} . Proof. 1. By assumption, A ∼ A ′ ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A^{\prime}\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} for some α \alpha . So A ⇓ V A\Downarrow V , A ′ ⇓ V ′ A^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime} , and τ ω ​ ( V , V ′ , α ) \tau_{\omega}(V,V^{\prime},\alpha) . Since _ ↦ _ \_\mapsto\_ is deterministic, A ′′ ⇓ V A^{\prime\prime}\Downarrow V , and the result holds. 2. By assumption, M ∼ M ′ ∈ α M\sim M^{\prime}\in\alpha for some α \alpha where A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} . It suffices to show M ′′ ∼ M ′ ∈ α M^{\prime\prime}\sim M^{\prime}\in\alpha . By assumption, M ⇓ V M\Downarrow V , M ′ ⇓ V ′ M^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime} , and α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) . Hence M ′′ ⇓ V M^{\prime\prime}\Downarrow V , and we are done. 3. By assumption, M ∼ M ′ ∈ α M\sim M^{\prime}\in\alpha P for some α \alpha where A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} . This means that (a) M ⇓ c V M\Downarrow^{c}V (b) M ′ ⇓ c ′ V ′ M^{\prime}\Downarrow^{c^{\prime}}V^{\prime} (c) P ⇓ p ¯ P\Downarrow\bar{p} (d) p ≥ max ⁡ ( c , c ′ ) p\geq\max{(c,c^{\prime})} (e) α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) It suffices to show M ′′ ∼ M ′ ∈ α ⁡ [ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( P ) ] M^{\prime\prime}\sim M^{\prime}\in\alpha\;{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{[{\mathtt{suc}}(P)]}} . Hence we have M ′′ ⇓ c + 1 V M^{\prime\prime}\Downarrow^{c+1}V and 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( P ) ⇓ p + 1 ¯ {\mathtt{suc}}(P)\Downarrow\bar{p+1} . It suffices to show p + 1 ≥ max ⁡ ( c + 1 , c ′ ) p+1\geq\max{(c+1,c^{\prime})} , which holds. 4. Similar to above. 5. By assumption, P ∼ P ′ ∈ ω P\sim P^{\prime}\in\omega , which means P ⇓ p ¯ P\Downarrow\bar{p} , P ′ ⇓ p ′ ¯ P^{\prime}\Downarrow\bar{p^{\prime}} , and ω ⁡ ( p ¯ , p ′ ¯ ) \omega(\bar{p},\bar{p^{\prime}}) . It suffices to show p = p ′ p=p^{\prime} , which holds by definition of ω \omega . ∎ Open Judgments To extend the closed judgments to open terms, we define Γ ​ 𝗍𝖾𝗅𝖾 \Gamma\;\mathsf{tele} , which represent telescopes. Definition 5.2 (Telescopes) . 1. ⋅ 𝗍𝖾𝗅𝖾 \cdot\;\mathsf{tele} . 2. Γ , x : A ​ 𝗍𝖾𝗅𝖾 \Gamma,x{:}A\;\mathsf{tele} when Γ ​ 𝗍𝖾𝗅𝖾 \Gamma\;\mathsf{tele} and A ∈ 𝖤𝗑𝗉 A\in\mathsf{Exp} . Open judgments are given meaning by functionality: open types are equal if all equal substitution instances are equal as closed types, and similarly for membership. As before, substitution instances range over values. Definition 5.3 (Equal Instances) . Given Γ ​ 𝗍𝖾𝗅𝖾 \Gamma\;\mathsf{tele} , define γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma : 1. ⋅ ∼ ⋅ ∈ ⋅ \cdot\sim\cdot\in\cdot . 2. γ , [ a ↦ V ] ∼ γ ′ , [ a ↦ V ′ ] ∈ Γ , a : A \gamma,[a\mapsto V]\sim\gamma^{\prime},[a\mapsto V^{\prime}]\in\Gamma,a{:}A when V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 γ ^ A V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}A and γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma . Definition 5.4 (Open Judgments) . 1. Γ ≫ A ≐ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A\doteq A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} when for all γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma , γ ^ ​ A ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}A\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} . 2. Γ ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ A \Gamma\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A when for all γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma , γ ^ ​ M ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ M ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ A \hat{\gamma}M\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}A . 3. Γ ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] \Gamma\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} when for all γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma , γ ^ ​ M ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ M ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ A ​ [ γ ^ ​ P ] \hat{\gamma}M\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\hat{\gamma}P]}} . 4. Γ ≫ V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 A \Gamma\gg V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}A when for all γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma , γ ^ V ≐ γ ^ ′ V ′ ∈ 0 γ ^ A \hat{\gamma}V\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}V^{\prime}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}A . Proposition 5.4 (Conversion) . Γ ≫ V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 A \Gamma\gg V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}A ⇔ \iff Γ ≫ V ≐ V ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 ] \Gamma\gg V\doteq V^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[{\mathtt{zero}}]}} . In order to prove some structural lemmas about the open judgments (such as symmetry and transitivity), we need to restrict Γ \Gamma to telescopes that are also contexts : Definition 5.5 (Contexts) . Given Γ ​ 𝗍𝖾𝗅𝖾 \Gamma\;\mathsf{tele} , and Γ ′ ​ 𝗍𝖾𝗅𝖾 \Gamma^{\prime}\;\mathsf{tele} , define Γ ≐ Γ ′ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma\doteq\Gamma^{\prime}\;\mathsf{ctx} : 1. ⋅ ≐ ⋅ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \cdot\doteq\cdot\;\mathsf{ctx} . 2. Γ , a : A ≐ Γ ′ , a : A ′ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma,a{:}A\doteq\Gamma^{\prime},a{:}A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{ctx} when Γ ≫ A ≐ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A\doteq A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} and Γ ≐ Γ ′ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma\doteq\Gamma^{\prime}\;\mathsf{ctx} . Lemma 5.5 (PER of contexts) . Given Γ ≐ Γ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma\doteq\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx} , 1. If γ 1 ∼ γ 2 ∈ Γ \gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma , then γ 2 ∼ γ 1 ∈ Γ \gamma_{2}\sim\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma . 2. If γ 1 ∼ γ 2 ∈ Γ \gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma and γ 2 ∼ γ 3 ∈ Γ \gamma_{2}\sim\gamma_{3}\in\Gamma , then γ 1 ∼ γ 3 ∈ Γ \gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{3}\in\Gamma . Proof. Part 1): induction on the length of Γ \Gamma . If Γ = ⋅ \Gamma=\cdot , then the result follows by definition. Otherwise, Γ = Γ ′ , a : A \Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime},a{:}A , and suppose γ 1 , [ a ↦ V 1 ] ∼ γ 2 , [ a ↦ V 2 ] ∈ Γ ′ , a : A \gamma_{1},[a\mapsto V_{1}]\sim\gamma_{2},[a\mapsto V_{2}]\in\Gamma^{\prime},a{:}A . We need to show γ 2 , [ a ↦ V 2 ] ∼ γ 1 , [ a ↦ V 1 ] ∈ Γ ′ , a : A \gamma_{2},[a\mapsto V_{2}]\sim\gamma_{1},[a\mapsto V_{1}]\in\Gamma^{\prime},a{:}A . By definition, it suffices to show 1. V 2 ≐ V 1 ∈ 0 γ ^ 2 A V_{2}\doteq V_{1}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}_{2}A 2. γ 2 ∼ γ 1 ∈ Γ ′ \gamma_{2}\sim\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma^{\prime} By the assumption that Γ ≐ Γ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma\doteq\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx} , we have Γ ′ ≫ A ≐ A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma^{\prime}\gg A\doteq A\;\mathsf{type} and Γ ′ ≐ Γ ′ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma^{\prime}\doteq\Gamma^{\prime}\;\mathsf{ctx} . By assumption, we have V 1 ≐ V 2 ∈ 0 γ ^ 1 A V_{1}\doteq V_{2}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}_{1}A and γ 1 ∼ γ 2 ∈ Γ ′ \gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma^{\prime} . Hence the second obligation follows by induction. The first obligation follows from Lemma 5.1 and 5.2 , given that we show γ ^ 1 ​ A ≐ γ ^ 2 ​ A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}_{1}A\doteq\hat{\gamma}_{2}A\;\mathsf{type} , which in turn follows by definition of Γ ′ ≫ A ≐ A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma^{\prime}\gg A\doteq A\;\mathsf{type} . Part 2): induction on the length of Γ \Gamma . If Γ = ⋅ \Gamma=\cdot , then the result is immediate. Otherwise, Γ = Γ ′ , a : A \Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime},a{:}A . Suppose γ 1 , [ a ↦ V 1 ] ∼ γ 2 , [ a ↦ V 2 ] ∈ Γ ′ , a : A \gamma_{1},[a\mapsto V_{1}]\sim\gamma_{2},[a\mapsto V_{2}]\in\Gamma^{\prime},a{:}A and γ 2 , [ a ↦ V 2 ] ∼ γ 3 , [ a ↦ V 3 ] ∈ Γ ′ , a : A \gamma_{2},[a\mapsto V_{2}]\sim\gamma_{3},[a\mapsto V_{3}]\in\Gamma^{\prime},a{:}A . We need to show γ 1 , [ a ↦ V 1 ] ∼ γ 3 , [ a ↦ V 3 ] ∈ Γ ′ , a : A \gamma_{1},[a\mapsto V_{1}]\sim\gamma_{3},[a\mapsto V_{3}]\in\Gamma^{\prime},a{:}A . By definition, it suffices to show 1. V 1 ≐ V 3 ∈ 0 γ ^ 1 A V_{1}\doteq V_{3}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}_{1}A 2. γ 1 ∼ γ 3 ∈ Γ ′ \gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{3}\in\Gamma^{\prime} By the assumptions, we have 1. Γ ′ ≐ Γ ′ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma^{\prime}\doteq\Gamma^{\prime}\;\mathsf{ctx} 2. Γ ′ ≫ A ≐ A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma^{\prime}\gg A\doteq A\;\mathsf{type} 3. V 1 ≐ V 2 ∈ 0 γ ^ 1 A V_{1}\doteq V_{2}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}_{1}A 4. γ 1 ∼ γ 2 ∈ Γ ′ \gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma^{\prime} 5. V 2 ≐ V 3 ∈ 0 γ ^ 2 A V_{2}\doteq V_{3}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}_{2}A 6. γ 2 ∼ γ 3 ∈ Γ ′ \gamma_{2}\sim\gamma_{3}\in\Gamma^{\prime} Hence we have γ ^ 1 ​ A ≐ γ ^ 2 ​ A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}_{1}A\doteq\hat{\gamma}_{2}A\;\mathsf{type} . By Lemma 5.2 , this implies V 2 ≐ V 3 ∈ 0 γ ^ 1 A V_{2}\doteq V_{3}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}_{1}A , and by Lemma 5.1 we get V 1 ≐ V 3 ∈ 0 γ ^ 1 A V_{1}\doteq V_{3}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}_{1}A . Since the latter obligation follows by induction, the result holds. ∎ Lemma 5.6 (PER of open judgments) . Given Γ ≐ Γ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma\doteq\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx} , 1. Γ ≫ _ ≐ _ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg\_\doteq\_\;\mathsf{type} is a PER. 2. If Γ ≫ A ≐ A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A\doteq A\;\mathsf{type} , then Γ ≫ _ ≐ _ ∈ A \Gamma\gg\_\doteq\_\in A is a PER. 3. If Γ ≫ A ≐ A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A\doteq A\;\mathsf{type} and Γ ≫ P ≐ P ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 \Gamma\gg P\doteq P\in\mathtt{nat} , then Γ ≫ _ ≐ _ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] \Gamma\gg\_\doteq\_\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} is a PER. Proof. For part 1), suppose Γ ≫ A 1 ≐ A 2 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A_{1}\doteq A_{2}\;\mathsf{type} . We need to show Γ ≫ A 2 ≐ A 1 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A_{2}\doteq A_{1}\;\mathsf{type} . Let γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma . It suffices to show γ ^ ​ A 2 ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ A 1 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}A_{2}\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}A_{1}\;\mathsf{type} . By Lemma 5.5 , γ ′ ∼ γ ∈ Γ \gamma^{\prime}\sim\gamma\in\Gamma , so by assumption, γ ^ ′ ​ A 1 ≐ γ ^ ​ A 2 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}^{\prime}A_{1}\doteq\hat{\gamma}A_{2}\;\mathsf{type} . Hence the result follows by Lemma 5.1 . Now suppose Γ ≫ A 1 ≐ A 2 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A_{1}\doteq A_{2}\;\mathsf{type} and Γ ≫ A 2 ≐ A 3 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A_{2}\doteq A_{3}\;\mathsf{type} . We need to show Γ ≫ A 1 ≐ A 3 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A_{1}\doteq A_{3}\;\mathsf{type} . Let γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma . It suffices to show γ ^ ​ A 1 ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ A 3 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}A_{1}\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}A_{3}\;\mathsf{type} . By Lemma 5.5 , γ ∼ γ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma\in\Gamma , so by assumption, γ ^ ​ A 1 ≐ γ ^ ​ A 2 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}A_{1}\doteq\hat{\gamma}A_{2}\;\mathsf{type} and γ ^ ​ A 2 ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ A 3 ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}A_{2}\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}A_{3}\;\mathsf{type} . Hence the result follows by Lemma 5.1 . Part 2) - 3) follow similarly. ∎ Head expansion can be extended to open judgments. Write Γ ≫ E ↦ E ′ \Gamma\gg E\mapsto E^{\prime} if for all γ ∼ γ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma\in\Gamma , γ ^ E ↦ ∗ γ ^ E ′ \hat{\gamma}E\mapsto^{*}\hat{\gamma}E^{\prime} . Lemma 5.7 (Open head expansion) . Given Γ ≐ Γ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma\doteq\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx} , 1. If Γ ≫ A ≐ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A\doteq A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} and Γ ≫ A ′′ ↦ A \Gamma\gg A^{\prime\prime}\mapsto A , then Γ ≫ A ′′ ≐ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg A^{\prime\prime}\doteq A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} . 2. If Γ ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ A \Gamma\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A and Γ ≫ M ′′ ↦ M \Gamma\gg M^{\prime\prime}\mapsto M , then Γ ≫ M ′′ ≐ M ′ ∈ A \Gamma\gg M^{\prime\prime}\doteq M^{\prime}\in A . Proof. 1. Let γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma . We need to show γ ^ ​ A ′′ ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}A^{\prime\prime}\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} . By assumption, we know γ ^ ​ A ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}A\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} . Since Γ ≐ Γ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma\doteq\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx} , γ ∼ γ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma\in\Gamma , so we have γ ^ A ′′ ↦ ∗ γ ^ A \hat{\gamma}A^{\prime\prime}\mapsto^{*}\hat{\gamma}A , so the result follows from Lemma 5.3 . 2. Let γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma . We need to show γ ^ ​ M ′′ ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ M ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ A \hat{\gamma}M^{\prime\prime}\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}A . By assumption, we know γ ^ ​ M ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ M ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ A \hat{\gamma}M\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}A . Since Γ ≐ Γ ​ 𝖼𝗍𝗑 \Gamma\doteq\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx} , γ ∼ γ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma\in\Gamma , so we have γ ^ M ′′ ↦ ∗ γ ^ M \hat{\gamma}M^{\prime\prime}\mapsto^{*}\hat{\gamma}M , so the result follows from Lemma 5.3 . ∎ Open judgments admit the structural rules of identity, weakening, and substitution: Lemma 5.8 (Hypothesis) . Γ , a : A ≫ a ∈ A ⁡ [ 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 ] \Gamma,a{:}A\gg a\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[{\mathtt{zero}}]}} . Proof. Let γ , [ a ↦ V ] ∼ γ ′ , [ a ↦ V ′ ] ∈ Γ , a : A \gamma,[a\mapsto V]\sim\gamma^{\prime},[a\mapsto V^{\prime}]\in\Gamma,a{:}A . We need to show V ≐ V ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ A ​ [ 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 ] V\doteq V^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[{\mathtt{zero}}]}} , which holds by definition of γ , [ a ↦ V ] ∼ γ ′ , [ a ↦ V ′ ] ∈ Γ , a : A \gamma,[a\mapsto V]\sim\gamma^{\prime},[a\mapsto V^{\prime}]\in\Gamma,a{:}A . ∎ Lemma 5.9 (Weakening) . 1. If Γ ≫ B ≐ B ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg B\doteq B^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} , then Γ , a : A ≫ B ≐ B ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma,a{:}A\gg B\doteq B^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} . 2. If Γ ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ B \Gamma\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in B , then Γ , a : A ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ B \Gamma,a{:}A\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in B . 3. If Γ ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ B ⁡ [ P ] \Gamma\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in B{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} , then Γ , a : A ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ B ⁡ [ P ] \Gamma,a{:}A\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in B{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} . Proof. For part 1, let γ , [ a ↦ V ] ∼ γ ′ , [ a ↦ V ′ ] ∈ Γ , a : A \gamma,[a\mapsto V]\sim\gamma^{\prime},[a\mapsto V^{\prime}]\in\Gamma,a{:}A . We need to show γ ^ ​ B ≐ γ ^ ​ B ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}B\doteq\hat{\gamma}B^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} . By definition, γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma , so the result follows from assumption. Part 2 and 3 follow similarly. ∎ For sequencing operations occurring in types and cost specifications, we write ⟨ E 1 / a ⟩ ​ E 2 \left<E_{1}/a\right>E_{2} for 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = E 1 ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ E 2 \mathtt{let}\;a=E_{1}\;\mathtt{in}\;E_{2} . Remark . “leftist” substitution is required because untyped principles such as direct computation [ 43 ] does not hold in 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} . In contrast to NuPRL , types can internalize cost-aware judgments, and so in general membership is not invariant under untyped rewrite rules. Lemma 5.10 (Substitution) . 1. If Γ ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ A \Gamma\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A and Γ , a : A ≫ B ≐ B ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma,a{:}A\gg B\doteq B^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} , then Γ ≫ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ B ≐ ⟨ M ′ / a ⟩ ​ B ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \Gamma\gg\left<M/a\right>B\doteq\left<M^{\prime}/a\right>B^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} . 2. If Γ ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ A \Gamma\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A and Γ , a : A ≫ N ≐ N ′ ∈ B \Gamma,a{:}A\gg N\doteq N^{\prime}\in B , then Γ ≫ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ≐ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ′ ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ′ ∈ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ B \Gamma\gg\mathtt{let}\;a=M\;\mathtt{in}\;N\doteq\mathtt{let}\;a=M^{\prime}\;\mathtt{in}\;N^{\prime}\in\left<M/a\right>B . 3. If Γ ≫ M ≐ M ′ ∈ A ⁡ [ P ] \Gamma\gg M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P]}} and Γ , a : A ≫ N ≐ N ′ ∈ B ⁡ [ Q ] \Gamma,a{:}A\gg N\doteq N^{\prime}\in B{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[Q]}} , then Γ ≫ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ≐ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ′ ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ′ ∈ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ B ​ [ P ​ + ^ ​ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ​ ( ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ Q ) ] \Gamma\gg\mathtt{let}\;a=M\;\mathtt{in}\;N\doteq\mathtt{let}\;a=M^{\prime}\;\mathtt{in}\;N^{\prime}\in\left<M/a\right>B{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P\hat{+}{\mathtt{suc}}(\left<M/a\right>Q)]}} . Proof. 1. Let γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma . We need to show γ ^ ​ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ B ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ ⟨ M ′ / a ⟩ ​ B ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}\left<M/a\right>B\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}\left<M^{\prime}/a\right>B^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} . By assumption, we have γ ^ ​ M ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ M ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ A \hat{\gamma}M\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}A . This means that (a) γ ^ ​ M ⇓ V \hat{\gamma}M\Downarrow V (b) γ ^ ′ ​ M ′ ⇓ V ′ \hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime} and V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 γ ^ A V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}A . By definition, γ , [ a ↦ V ] ∼ γ ′ , [ a ↦ V ′ ] ∈ Γ , a : A \gamma,[a\mapsto V]\sim\gamma^{\prime},[a\mapsto V^{\prime}]\in\Gamma,a{:}A , so by assumption, γ ^ ​ [ V / a ] ​ B ≐ γ ^ ​ [ V ′ / a ] ​ B ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 \hat{\gamma}[V/a]B\doteq\hat{\gamma}[V^{\prime}/a]B^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} , and the result follows from Lemma 5.3 . 2. Let γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma . We need to show γ ^ ​ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ′ ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ B \hat{\gamma}\mathtt{let}\;a=M\;\mathtt{in}\;N\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}\mathtt{let}\;a=M^{\prime}\;\mathtt{in}\;N^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}\left<M/a\right>B . By assumption, we have γ ^ ​ M ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ M ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ A \hat{\gamma}M\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}A . This means that (a) γ ^ ​ M ⇓ V \hat{\gamma}M\Downarrow V (b) γ ^ ′ ​ M ′ ⇓ V ′ \hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime} and V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 γ ^ A V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}A . By definition, γ , [ a ↦ V ] ∼ γ ′ , [ a ↦ V ′ ] ∈ Γ , a : A \gamma,[a\mapsto V]\sim\gamma^{\prime},[a\mapsto V^{\prime}]\in\Gamma,a{:}A , so by assumption, γ ^ ​ [ V / a ] ​ N ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ [ V ′ / a ] ​ N ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ [ V / a ] ​ B \hat{\gamma}[V/a]N\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}[V^{\prime}/a]N^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}[V/a]B , and the result follows from Lemma 5.3 . 3. Let γ ∼ γ ′ ∈ Γ \gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma . We need to show γ ^ ​ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ′ ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ B ​ [ γ ^ ​ P ​ + ^ ​ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ​ ( ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ Q ) ] \hat{\gamma}\mathtt{let}\;a=M\;\mathtt{in}\;N\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}\mathtt{let}\;a=M^{\prime}\;\mathtt{in}\;N^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}\left<M/a\right>B{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\hat{\gamma}P\hat{+}{\mathtt{suc}}(\left<M/a\right>Q)]}} . By assumption, we have γ ^ ​ M ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ M ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ A ​ [ γ ^ ​ P ] \hat{\gamma}M\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}A{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\hat{\gamma}P]}} . This means that (a) γ ^ M ⇓ c V \hat{\gamma}M\Downarrow^{c}V (b) γ ^ ′ M ′ ⇓ c ′ V ′ \hat{\gamma}^{\prime}M^{\prime}\Downarrow^{c^{\prime}}V^{\prime} (c) γ ^ ​ P ⇓ p ¯ \hat{\gamma}P\Downarrow\bar{p} (d) p ≥ max ⁡ ( c , c ′ ) p\geq\max(c,c^{\prime}) Hence V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 γ ^ A V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}\hat{\gamma}A . By definition, γ , [ a ↦ V ] ∼ γ ′ , [ a ↦ V ′ ] ∈ Γ , a : A \gamma,[a\mapsto V]\sim\gamma^{\prime},[a\mapsto V^{\prime}]\in\Gamma,a{:}A , so by assumption, γ ^ ​ [ V / a ] ​ N ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ [ V ′ / a ] ​ N ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ [ V / a ] ​ B ​ [ γ ^ ​ [ V / a ] ​ Q ] \hat{\gamma}[V/a]N\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}[V^{\prime}/a]N^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}[V/a]B{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\hat{\gamma}[V/a]Q]}} . By Lemma 5.3 , we have γ ^ ​ [ V / a ] ​ N ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ [ V ′ / a ] ​ N ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ B ​ [ γ ^ ​ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ Q ] \hat{\gamma}[V/a]N\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}[V^{\prime}/a]N^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}\left<M/a\right>B{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\hat{\gamma}\left<M/a\right>Q]}} . By Lemma 5.3 , we have γ ^ ​ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ≐ γ ^ ′ ​ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ a = M ′ ​ 𝚒𝚗 ​ N ′ ∈ γ ^ ​ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ B ​ [ γ ^ ​ P ​ + ^ ​ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ​ ( γ ^ ​ ⟨ M / a ⟩ ​ Q ) ] \hat{\gamma}\mathtt{let}\;a=M\;\mathtt{in}\;N\doteq\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}\mathtt{let}\;a=M^{\prime}\;\mathtt{in}\;N^{\prime}\in\hat{\gamma}\left<M/a\right>B{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\hat{\gamma}P\hat{+}{\mathtt{suc}}(\hat{\gamma}\left<M/a\right>Q)]}} , as required. ∎ Semantics of types Since type systems satisfy unicity, it is sometimes useful to refer to the denotation of types, i.e. the unique PER designated as its membership relation. Lemma 5.11 (Closed denotation) . If A ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 A\;\mathsf{type} , then there is a unique α \alpha such that A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} . Write this as α = ⟦ A ⟧ \alpha=\llbracket A\rrbracket . Proof. By unicity of τ ω \tau_{\omega} . ∎ Lemma 5.12 (Open denotation) . Given A ∼ A ′ ↓ α ∈ τ i A\sim A^{\prime}\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{i} and a : A ≫ B ≐ B ′ ∈ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i a{:}A\gg B\doteq B^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Univ}_{i} , there is a β \beta such that a : α ​ ⊳ ​ B ∼ B ′ ↓ β ∈ τ i a{:}\alpha\rhd B\sim B^{\prime}\downarrow\beta\in\tau_{i} . Write this as β = ⟦ a : α . ( B , B ′ ) ⟧ \beta=\llbracket a{:}\alpha.(B,B^{\prime})\rrbracket . Proof. First, we need to construct β \beta . Let V , V ′ V,V^{\prime} such that α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) . By assumption, we have [ V / a ] ​ B ≐ [ V ′ / a ] ​ B ′ ∈ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i [V/a]B\doteq[V^{\prime}/a]B^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Univ}_{i} . This means [ V / a ] ​ B ∼ [ V ′ / a ] ​ B ′ ↓ ϕ ∈ τ i [V/a]B\sim[V^{\prime}/a]B^{\prime}\downarrow\phi\in\tau_{i} for some ϕ \phi . Let β = ( V , V ′ ) ↦ ϕ \beta=(V,V^{\prime})\mapsto\phi . We need to show a : α ​ ⊳ ​ B ∼ B ′ ↓ β ∈ τ i a{:}\alpha\rhd B\sim B^{\prime}\downarrow\beta\in\tau_{i} . Let V , V ′ V,V^{\prime} such that α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) . It suffices to show [ V / a ] ​ B ∼ [ V ′ / a ] ​ B ′ ↓ β V , V ′ ∈ τ i [V/a]B\sim[V^{\prime}/a]B^{\prime}\downarrow\beta_{V,V^{\prime}}\in\tau_{i} , which holds by definition of β \beta . ∎ Lemma 5.13 (Functionality) . If a : α ​ ⊳ ​ B ∼ B ′ ↓ β ∈ τ ω a{:}\alpha\rhd B\sim B^{\prime}\downarrow\beta\in\tau_{\omega} , then β V , V = β V , V ′ \beta_{V,V}=\beta_{V,V^{\prime}} for all α ⁡ ( V , V ) \alpha(V,V) and α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) . Proof. Let V , V ′ V,V^{\prime} such that α ⁡ ( V , V ) \alpha(V,V) and α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) . By assumption, we have 1. [ V / a ] ​ B ∼ [ V / a ] ​ B ′ ↓ β V , V ∈ τ ω [V/a]B\sim[V/a]B^{\prime}\downarrow\beta_{V,V}\in\tau_{\omega} 2. [ V / a ] ​ B ∼ [ V ′ / a ] ​ B ′ ↓ β V , V ′ ∈ τ ω [V/a]B\sim[V^{\prime}/a]B^{\prime}\downarrow\beta_{V,V^{\prime}}\in\tau_{\omega} Apply symmetry and transitivity: [ V / a ] ​ B ′ ∼ [ V / a ] ​ B ↓ β V , V ∈ τ ω \displaystyle[V/a]B^{\prime}\sim[V/a]B\downarrow\beta_{V,V}\in\tau_{\omega} [ V / a ] ​ B ∼ [ V / a ] ​ B ↓ β V , V ∈ τ ω \displaystyle[V/a]B\sim[V/a]B\downarrow\beta_{V,V}\in\tau_{\omega} (5.1) [ V ′ / a ] ​ B ′ ∼ [ V / a ] ​ B ↓ β V , V ′ ∈ τ ω \displaystyle[V^{\prime}/a]B^{\prime}\sim[V/a]B\downarrow\beta_{V,V^{\prime}}\in\tau_{\omega} [ V / a ] ​ B ∼ [ V / a ] ​ B ↓ β V , V ′ ∈ τ ω \displaystyle[V/a]B\sim[V/a]B\downarrow\beta_{V,V^{\prime}}\in\tau_{\omega} (5.2) Thus by unicity with ( 5.1 ) and ( 5.2 ) we have β V , V = β V , V ′ \beta_{V,V}=\beta_{V,V^{\prime}} . ∎ Lemma 5.14 (Open computation) . Given A ∼ A ′ ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A^{\prime}\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} , a : A ≫ B ≐ B ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 a{:}A\gg B\doteq B\;\mathsf{type} , and a : A ≫ N ≐ N ′ ∈ B a{:}A\gg N\doteq N^{\prime}\in B , there is a β \beta such that a : α ​ ⊳ ​ B ∼ B ′ ↓ β ∈ τ ω a{:}\alpha\rhd B\sim B^{\prime}\downarrow\beta\in\tau_{\omega} and a : α ​ ⊳ ​ N ∼ N ′ ∈ β a{:}\alpha\rhd N\sim N^{\prime}\in\beta . Proof. The first part is the same as in Lemma 5.12 . For the latter, let V , V ′ V,V^{\prime} such that α ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \alpha(V,V^{\prime}) . We need to show [ V / a ] ​ N ∼ [ V ′ / a ] ​ N ′ ∈ β V , V ′ [V/a]N\sim[V^{\prime}/a]N^{\prime}\in\beta_{V,V^{\prime}} . By assumption, [ V / a ] ​ N ≐ [ V ′ / a ] ​ N ′ ∈ [ V / a ] ​ B [V/a]N\doteq[V^{\prime}/a]N^{\prime}\in[V/a]B . So it suffices to show ⟦ [ V / a ] ​ B ⟧ = β V , V ′ \llbracket[V/a]B\rrbracket=\beta_{V,V^{\prime}} , which follows from Lemma 5.13 . ∎ 6 Semantic Proof Theory In the context of computational type theories, a proof theory could be viewed as a collection of generally useful lemmas about the semantic construction. As such, it is not the least set of derivable facts from the collection, and issues of admissibility of rules do not arise in this setting. This is a pragmatic approach, as it is often convenient to extend the proof theory as needed with new lemmas. Here we present a particular proof theory for 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} . Notice that even though the lemmas are written in an inference rule format, they should be read as “if the premises are true, then the conclusions are true”, i.e., not an inductive definition. In Figure 4 , we present a collection of lemmas that is useful for complexity analysis. They can be grouped roughly by the type constructor involved, resulting in the usual formation, introduction, and elimination rules. For brevity, we write the rules in the local form; however, all rules (with the exception of Lemma 6.14 , 6.16 , and 6.17 ) can be interpreted to have an ambient context prepended to the judgments. Figure 5: A proof theory for 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} . 6.1 Universes The introduction rules for 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i \mathtt{Univ}_{i} are the formation rules for the “small types” ( Lemma 6.6 , 6.18 , 6.12 , 6.15 , 6.21 ). These introduction rules capture the by-name evaluation behavior of elements of the universe; consequently, there is no cost-aware version of the typecase mechanism. Lemma 6.1 (Universe formation) . 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ≐ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ∈ 0 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i + 1 \mathtt{Univ}_{i}\doteq\mathtt{Univ}_{i}\in_{0}\mathtt{Univ}_{i+1} for all i i . Proof. First, we need to show there is a ϕ \phi such that 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i + 1 ∼ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i + 1 ↓ ϕ ∈ τ ω \mathtt{Univ}_{i+1}\sim\mathtt{Univ}_{i+1}\downarrow\phi\in\tau_{\omega} . By definition, τ ω = Types ​ ( v ω , τ ω ) \tau_{\omega}=\text{Types}(v_{\omega},\tau_{\omega}) and Univ ​ ( v ω ) ⊆ Types ​ ( v ω , τ ω ) \text{Univ}(v_{\omega})\subseteq\text{Types}(v_{\omega},\tau_{\omega}) , so it suffices to show 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i + 1 ∼ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i + 1 ↓ ϕ ∈ v ω \mathtt{Univ}_{i+1}\sim\mathtt{Univ}_{i+1}\downarrow\phi\in v_{\omega} for some ϕ \phi . By definition of v ω v_{\omega} , take ϕ = { ( A , B ) ∣ ∃ α . τ i + 1 ( A , B , α ) } \phi=\{(A,B)\mid\exists\alpha.\,\tau_{i+1}(A,B,\alpha)\} . Next, we need to show ϕ ⁡ ( 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i , 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ) \phi(\mathtt{Univ}_{i},\mathtt{Univ}_{i}) . It suffices to show there is α \alpha such that τ i + 1 ​ ( 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i , 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i , α ) \tau_{i+1}(\mathtt{Univ}_{i},\mathtt{Univ}_{i},\alpha) . Since τ i + 1 = Types ​ ( v i + 1 , τ i + 1 ) \tau_{i+1}=\text{Types}(v_{i+1},\tau_{i+1}) , it suffices to show 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ∼ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ↓ α ∈ v i + 1 \mathtt{Univ}_{i}\sim\mathtt{Univ}_{i}\downarrow\alpha\in v_{i+1} . By definition of v i + 1 v_{i+1} , it suffices to take α = { ( A , B ) ∣ ∃ β . τ i ( A , B , β ) } \alpha=\{(A,B)\mid\exists\beta.\,\tau_{i}(A,B,\beta)\} . ∎ The introduction rules for 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i \mathtt{Univ}_{i} are the formation rules for the type constructors. Lemma 6.2 (Universe elimination) . If 1. A ≐ A ′ ∈ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i A\doteq A^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Univ}_{i} then A ≐ A ′ ​ 𝗍𝗒𝗉𝖾 A\doteq A^{\prime}\;\mathsf{type} . Proof. We need to show A ∼ A ′ ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A^{\prime}\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} for some α \alpha . By assumption, A ∼ A ′ ∈ ⟦ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ⟧ A\sim A^{\prime}\in\llbracket\mathtt{Univ}_{i}\rrbracket . By definition, ⟦ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ⟧ = { ( A , B ) ∣ ∃ α . τ i ( A , B , α ) } \llbracket\mathtt{Univ}_{i}\rrbracket=\{(A,B)\mid\exists\alpha.\,\tau_{i}(A,B,\alpha)\} Hence A ∼ A ′ ↓ α ∈ τ i A\sim A^{\prime}\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{i} for some α \alpha , and so the result holds by Lemma 4.10 . ∎ 6.2 Equality Lemma 6.3 (Equality formation) . If 1. A ≐ A ′ ∈ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i A\doteq A^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Univ}_{i} 2. M ≐ M ′ ∈ A M\doteq M^{\prime}\in A 3. N ≐ N ′ ∈ A N\doteq N^{\prime}\in A then 𝚎𝚚 A ( M , N ) ≐ 𝚎𝚚 A ′ ( M ′ , N ′ ) ∈ 0 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i \mathtt{eq}_{A}(M,\allowbreak N)\doteq\mathtt{eq}_{A^{\prime}}(M^{\prime},\allowbreak N^{\prime})\in_{0}\mathtt{Univ}_{i} Proof. By definition, ⟦ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ⟧ = { ( A , B ) ∣ ∃ ϕ . τ i ( A , B , ϕ ) } \llbracket\mathtt{Univ}_{i}\rrbracket=\{(A,B)\mid\exists\phi.\,\tau_{i}(A,B,\phi)\} , so we need to show τ i ​ ( 𝚎𝚚 A ​ ( M , N ) , 𝚎𝚚 A ′ ​ ( M ′ , N ′ ) , ϕ ) \tau_{i}(\mathtt{eq}_{A}(M,\allowbreak N),\mathtt{eq}_{A^{\prime}}(M^{\prime},\allowbreak N^{\prime}),\phi) for some ϕ \phi . Since τ i = Types ​ ( v i , τ i ) \tau_{i}=\text{Types}(v_{i},\tau_{i}) , it suffices to show Eq ​ ( τ i ) ​ ( 𝚎𝚚 A ​ ( M , N ) , 𝚎𝚚 A ′ ​ ( M ′ , N ′ ) , ϕ ) \text{Eq}(\tau_{i})(\mathtt{eq}_{A}(M,\allowbreak N),\mathtt{eq}_{A^{\prime}}(M^{\prime},\allowbreak N^{\prime}),\phi) . By assumption and definition of ⟦ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ⟧ \llbracket\mathtt{Univ}_{i}\rrbracket , we have A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ i A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{i} for some α \alpha . Furthermore, by Lemma 4.10 and unicity of τ ω \tau_{\omega} , we have 1. M ∼ M ′ ∈ α M\sim M^{\prime}\in\alpha 2. N ∼ N ′ ∈ α N\sim N^{\prime}\in\alpha So the result holds by taking ϕ = { ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) ∣ M ∼ N ∈ α } \phi=\{(\star,\star)\mid M\sim N\in\alpha\} . ∎ Lemma 6.4 (Equality introduction) . If 1. M ≐ N ∈ A M\doteq N\in A then ⋆ ≐ ⋆ ∈ 0 𝚎𝚚 A ( M , N ) \star\doteq\star\in_{0}\mathtt{eq}_{A}(M,\allowbreak N) . Proof. First, we need to show 𝚎𝚚 A ​ ( M , N ) ∼ 𝚎𝚚 A ​ ( M , N ) ↓ ϕ ∈ τ ω \mathtt{eq}_{A}(M,\allowbreak N)\sim\mathtt{eq}_{A}(M,\allowbreak N)\downarrow\phi\in\tau_{\omega} for some ϕ \phi , which holds by Lemma 6.3 . By definition, ϕ = { ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) ∣ M ∼ N ∈ ⟦ A ⟧ } \phi=\{(\star,\star)\mid M\sim N\in\llbracket A\rrbracket\} . Next, we need to show ϕ ⁡ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) \phi(\star,\star) . It suffices to show M ∼ N ∈ ⟦ A ⟧ M\sim N\in\llbracket A\rrbracket , which holds by assumption. ∎ Lemma 6.5 (Equality elimination) . If 1. P ≐ P ′ ∈ 𝚎𝚚 A ​ ( M , N ) P\doteq P^{\prime}\in\mathtt{eq}_{A}(M,\allowbreak N) then M ≐ N ∈ A M\doteq N\in A . Proof. First, we need to show A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} . By assumption, we have 𝚎𝚚 A ​ ( M , N ) ∼ 𝚎𝚚 A ​ ( M , N ) ↓ ϕ ∈ τ ω \mathtt{eq}_{A}(M,\allowbreak N)\sim\mathtt{eq}_{A}(M,\allowbreak N)\downarrow\phi\in\tau_{\omega} for some ϕ \phi . This implies A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} . By unicity, we have ϕ = { ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) ∣ M ∼ N ∈ α } \phi=\{(\star,\star)\mid M\sim N\in\alpha\} . It remains to show M ∼ N ∈ α M\sim N\in\alpha . By assumption, P ∼ P ′ ∈ ϕ P\sim P^{\prime}\in\phi , so we have M ∼ N ∈ α M\sim N\in\alpha , as required. ∎ 6.3 Nat Before introducing judgments involving complexities, we need to prove some lemmas governing the construction and use of natural numbers. As we alluded to in section 2 , the complexity component of a judgment is simply a natural number in the programming language itself. Aside from the rules for eliminating foreign functions, there are also two elimination rules – Lemma 6.10 is the ordinary eliminator, and Lemma 6.11 is the eliminator that additionally tracks the complexity of the case analysis given the complexity of each branch. Note that there is no syntactic form for the recursor: it is an instance of general recursion. The associated induction rule is also an instance of the elimination rule for the funtime type (see Lemma 6.23 and 6.24 ). Lemma 6.6 (Nat formation) . 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ≐ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ∈ 0 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i \mathtt{nat}\doteq\mathtt{nat}\in_{0}\mathtt{Univ}_{i} . Proof. By definition of ⟦ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ⟧ \llbracket\mathtt{Univ}_{i}\rrbracket , it suffices to show τ i ​ ( 𝚗𝚊𝚝 , 𝚗𝚊𝚝 , α ) \tau_{i}(\mathtt{nat},\mathtt{nat},\alpha) for some α \alpha . Unfolding definitions, we have Nat ​ ( τ i ) ⊆ Types ​ ( v ω , τ i ) = τ i \text{Nat}(\tau_{i})\subseteq\text{Types}(v_{\omega},\tau_{i})=\tau_{i} . So take α = ω \alpha=\omega , since Nat ​ ( τ i ) ​ ( 𝚗𝚊𝚝 , 𝚗𝚊𝚝 , ω ) \text{Nat}(\tau_{i})(\mathtt{nat},\mathtt{nat},\omega) . ∎ Lemma 6.7 (Nat introduction) . 1. 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 ≐ 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 {\mathtt{zero}}\doteq{\mathtt{zero}}\in\mathtt{nat} . 2. If M ≐ M ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 M\doteq M^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat} , then 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( M ) ≐ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( M ′ ) ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 {\mathtt{suc}}(M)\doteq{\mathtt{suc}}(M^{\prime})\in\mathtt{nat} . Proof. First, by Lemma 6.6 , we know there is α \alpha such that 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ∼ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ↓ α ∈ τ \mathtt{nat}\sim\mathtt{nat}\downarrow\alpha\in\tau . By unicity of τ ω \tau_{\omega} , α = ω \alpha=\omega . For part 1, it suffices to show 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 ∼ 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 ∈ ω {\mathtt{zero}}\sim{\mathtt{zero}}\in\omega . This holds by definition, as ω = { ( 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 , 𝚣𝚎𝚛𝚘 ) } ∪ { ( 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( V ) , 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( V ′ ) ) ∣ ω ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) } \omega=\{({\mathtt{zero}},{\mathtt{zero}})\}\cup\{({\mathtt{suc}}(V),{\mathtt{suc}}(V^{\prime}))\mid\omega(V,V^{\prime})\} . Now suppose M ≐ M ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 M\doteq M^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat} , which by above, means M ∼ M ′ ∈ ω M\sim M^{\prime}\in\omega . We need to show 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( M ) ∼ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( M ′ ) ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 {\mathtt{suc}}(M)\sim{\mathtt{suc}}(M^{\prime})\in\mathtt{nat} . By assumption, M ⇓ V M\Downarrow V , M ′ ⇓ V ′ M^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime} , and ω ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \omega(V,V^{\prime}) . Hence 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( M ) ⇓ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( V ) {\mathtt{suc}}(M)\Downarrow{\mathtt{suc}}(V) and 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( M ′ ) ⇓ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( V ′ ) {\mathtt{suc}}(M^{\prime})\Downarrow{\mathtt{suc}}(V^{\prime}) . By definition, ω ⁡ ( 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( V ) , 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( V ′ ) ) \omega({\mathtt{suc}}(V),{\mathtt{suc}}(V^{\prime})) , and so 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( M ) ∼ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ⁡ ( M ′ ) ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 {\mathtt{suc}}(M)\sim{\mathtt{suc}}(M^{\prime})\in\mathtt{nat} . ∎ Lemma 6.8 (FF elimination (1)) . 1. If f : ℕ → ℕ f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N} and M ≐ M ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 M\doteq M^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat} , then f ^ ⁡ ( M ) ≐ f ^ ⁡ ( M ′ ) ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 \mathop{\hat{f}}(M)\doteq\mathop{\hat{f}}(M^{\prime})\in\mathtt{nat} . 2. If f : ℕ → ℕ → ℕ f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N} and M 1 ≐ M 1 ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 M_{1}\doteq M_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat} and M 2 ≐ M 2 ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 M_{2}\doteq M_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat} , then ( M 1 f ^ M 2 ) ≐ ( M 1 ′ f ^ M 2 ′ ) ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 (M_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{f}}M_{2})\doteq(M_{1}^{\prime}\mathbin{\widehat{f}}M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathtt{nat} . Proof. We prove the unary case, as the binary case follows similarly. Since 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ∼ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ↓ ω ∈ τ ω \mathtt{nat}\sim\mathtt{nat}\downarrow\omega\in\tau_{\omega} , it suffices to show f ^ ⁡ ( M ) ∼ f ^ ⁡ ( M ′ ) ∈ ω \mathop{\hat{f}}(M)\sim\mathop{\hat{f}}(M^{\prime})\in\omega . By assumption and Lemma 5.11 , we know M ⇓ V M\Downarrow V , M ′ ⇓ V ′ M^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime} , and ω ⁡ ( V , V ′ ) \omega(V,V^{\prime}) . This means V = m ¯ V=\bar{m} and V ′ = m ¯ V^{\prime}=\bar{m} for some m m . Hence, f ^ ⁡ ( M ) \displaystyle\mathop{\hat{f}}(M) ↦ f ^ ∗ ⁡ ( m ¯ ) ↦ f ⁡ ( m ) ¯ \displaystyle\mapsto^{*}\mathop{\hat{f}}(\bar{m})\mapsto\overline{f(m)} f ^ ⁡ ( M ′ ) \displaystyle\mathop{\hat{f}}(M^{\prime}) ↦ f ^ ∗ ⁡ ( m ¯ ) ↦ f ⁡ ( m ) ¯ \displaystyle\mapsto^{*}\mathop{\hat{f}}(\bar{m})\mapsto\overline{f(m)} By Lemma 4.1 , it suffices to show f ⁡ ( m ) ¯ ∼ f ⁡ ( m ) ¯ ∈ ω \overline{f(m)}\sim\overline{f(m)}\in\omega , which holds by definition of ω \omega . ∎ Lemma 6.9 (FF elimination (2)) . 1. If f : ℕ → ℕ f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N} and V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 𝚗𝚊𝚝 V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}\mathtt{nat} , then f ^ ⁡ ( V ) ≐ f ^ ⁡ ( V ′ ) ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ⁡ [ 1 ¯ ] \mathop{\hat{f}}(V)\doteq\mathop{\hat{f}}(V^{\prime})\in\mathtt{nat}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\bar{1}]}} . 2. If f : ℕ → ℕ → ℕ f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N} and V 1 ≐ V 1 ′ ∈ 0 𝚗𝚊𝚝 V_{1}\doteq V_{1}^{\prime}\in_{0}\mathtt{nat} and V 2 ≐ V 2 ′ ∈ 0 𝚗𝚊𝚝 V_{2}\doteq V_{2}^{\prime}\in_{0}\mathtt{nat} , then ( V 1 f ^ V 2 ) ≐ ( V 1 ′ f ^ V 2 ′ ) ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ⁡ [ 1 ¯ ] (V_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{f}}V_{2})\doteq(V_{1}^{\prime}\mathbin{\widehat{f}}V_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathtt{nat}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\bar{1}]}} . Proof. Similar to proof of Lemma 6.8 . ∎ Lemma 6.10 (Nat elimination (1)) . If 1. a : 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ≫ A ∈ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i a{:}\mathtt{nat}\gg A\in\mathtt{Univ}_{i} , 2. V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 { a : A ∣ B } V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}\{a{:}A\mid B\} then V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 A V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}A and there exists U , U ′ U,U^{\prime} such that U ≐ U ′ ∈ 0 [ V / a ] B U\doteq U^{\prime}\in_{0}[V/a]B . This implies there exists α , β \alpha,\beta such that A ∼ A ↓ α ∈ τ ω A\sim A\downarrow\alpha\in\tau_{\omega} and a : α ​ ⊳ ​ B ∼ B ↓ β ∈ τ ω a{:}\alpha\rhd B\sim B\downarrow\beta\in\tau_{\omega} . Hence we have V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 A V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}A . For the second part, we first need to show [ V / a ] ​ B ∼ [ V / a ] ​ B ↓ ρ ∈ τ ω [V/a]B\sim[V/a]B\downarrow\rho\in\tau_{\omega} for some ρ \rho . Taking ρ = β V , V ′ \rho=\beta_{V,V^{\prime}} , this follows from a : α ​ ⊳ ​ B ∼ B ↓ β ∈ τ ω a{:}\alpha\rhd B\sim B\downarrow\beta\in\tau_{\omega} and symmetry and transitivity of τ ω \tau_{\omega} . The fact that U ∼ U ′ ∈ ρ U\sim U^{\prime}\in\rho then holds by definition of ϕ \phi . ∎ 6.5 Relations Some properties about natural numbers can be lifted from the metatheory. Given a relation r : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℕ × ℕ ) r:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}) , M r ^ N M\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N is the internal representation of r ⁡ ( m , n ) r(m,n) when M ⇓ m ¯ M\Downarrow\bar{m} and N ⇓ n ¯ N\Downarrow\bar{n} . For the following lemmas, we just prove the binary versions, noting that the proof stays the same for any relation 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℕ k ) \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^{k}) of fixed arity k k . As for subsets, the rules for relations cannot be generalized to arbitrary contexts in the expected way. The introduction rule (Lemma 6.16 ) involves identifying metatheoretic numbers that represent closed theoretic natural numbers. This is not possible for open terms of type 𝚗𝚊𝚝 \mathtt{nat} . The elimination rule (Lemma 6.17 ) has a similar problem. As in the case for subsets, we just instantiate the semantics involving open judgments for relations. Lemma 6.15 (Relation Formation) . If 1. r : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℕ × ℕ ) r:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}) 2. M ≐ M ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 M\doteq M^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat} 3. N ≐ N ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 N\doteq N^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat} then M r ^ N ≐ M ′ r ^ N ′ ∈ 0 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i M\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N\doteq M^{\prime}\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N^{\prime}\in_{0}\mathtt{Univ}_{i} . Proof. By definition of ⟦ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i ⟧ \llbracket\mathtt{Univ}_{i}\rrbracket , we need to show τ i ​ ( M r ^ N , M ′ r ^ N ′ , ϕ ) \tau_{i}(M\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N,M^{\prime}\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N^{\prime},\phi) for some ϕ \phi . By definition, τ i = Types ​ ( v i , τ i ) \tau_{i}=\text{Types}(v_{i},\tau_{i}) , so it suffices to show Rel2 ​ ( τ i ) ​ ( M r ^ N , M ′ r ^ N ′ , ϕ ) \text{Rel2}(\tau_{i})(M\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N,M^{\prime}\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N^{\prime},\phi) for some ϕ \phi . By assumption and unicity of τ ω \tau_{\omega} , we know M ∼ M ′ ∈ ω M\sim M^{\prime}\in\omega and N ∼ N ′ ∈ ω N\sim N^{\prime}\in\omega , so by definition, take ϕ = { ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) ∣ ∃ m , n . r ( m , n ) ∧ M ∼ m ¯ ∈ ω ∧ N ∼ n ¯ ∈ ω } \phi=\{(\star,\star)\mid\exists m,n.\,r(m,n)\land M\sim\bar{m}\in\omega\land N\sim\bar{n}\in\omega\} . ∎ Lemma 6.16 (Relation introduction) . If 1. M ≐ m ¯ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 M\doteq\bar{m}\in\mathtt{nat} for some m m 2. N ≐ n ¯ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 N\doteq\bar{n}\in\mathtt{nat} for some n n 3. r ⁡ ( m , n ) r(m,n) then ⋆ ≐ ⋆ ∈ 0 M r ^ N \star\doteq\star\in_{0}M\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N . Proof. First, we need to show M r ^ N ∼ M r ^ N ↓ ϕ ∈ τ ω M\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N\sim M\mathrel{\widehat{r}}N\downarrow\phi\in\tau_{\omega} for some ϕ \phi , which holds by Lemma 6.15 . By unicity, ϕ = { ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) ∣ ∃ m , n . r ( m , n ) ∧ M ∼ m ¯ ∈ ω ∧ N ∼ n ¯ ∈ ω } \phi=\{(\star,\star)\mid\exists m,n.\,r(m,n)\land M\sim\bar{m}\in\omega\land N\sim\bar{n}\in\omega\} Next, we need to show ϕ ⁡ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) \phi(\star,\star) . It suffices to show r ⁡ ( m , n ) r(m,n) , M ∼ m ¯ ∈ ω M\sim\bar{m}\in\omega , and N ∼ n ¯ ∈ ω N\sim\bar{n}\in\omega for some m , n m,n , which follows from the assumptions. ∎ Lemma 6.17 (Relation elimination) . If 1. Lemma 6.21 (Funtime formation) . If 1. A ≐ A ′ ∈ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i A\doteq A^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Univ}_{i} 2. a : A ≫ B ≐ B ′ ∈ 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i a{:}A\gg B\doteq B^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Univ}_{i} 3. a : A ≫ P ≐ P ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 a{:}A\gg P\doteq P^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat} then a : A → B [ P ] ≐ a : A ′ → B ′ [ P ′ ] ∈ 0 𝚄𝚗𝚒𝚟 i {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a{:}A\to B}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{[P]}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a{:}A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{[P^{\prime}]}}\in_{0}\mathtt{Univ}_{i} . By Lemma 5.14 , a : α ​ ⊳ ​ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ∼ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ↓ ρ ∈ τ ω a{:}\alpha\rhd\mathtt{nat}\sim\mathtt{nat}\downarrow\rho\in\tau_{\omega} and a : α ​ ⊳ ​ P ∼ P ′ ∈ ρ a{:}\alpha\rhd P\sim P^{\prime}\in\rho . By unicity ρ = ω \rho=\omega . Then by definition, we can pick ϕ = { ( 𝚏𝚞𝚗 f a 𝚒𝚜 N , 𝚏𝚞𝚗 f ′ a ′ 𝚒𝚜 N ′ ) ∣ a : α ⊳ [ 𝚏𝚞𝚗 f a 𝚒𝚜 N / f ] N ∼ [ 𝚏𝚞𝚗 f a 𝚒𝚜 N ′ / f ] N ′ ∈ β [ P ] } \phi=\{(\mathtt{fun}\,f\,a\,\mathtt{is}\,N,\mathtt{fun}\,f^{\prime}\,a^{\prime}\,\mathtt{is}\,N^{\prime})\mid a{:}\alpha\rhd[\mathtt{fun}\,f\,a\,\mathtt{is}\,N/f]N\sim[\mathtt{fun}\,f\,a\,\mathtt{is}\,N^{\prime}/f]N^{\prime}\in\beta\;{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{[P]}}{}\} . ∎ Before we prove the introduction rule, we will need some auxiliary definitions: 1. Let the domain of a PER α \alpha be | α | = { V ∣ α ⁡ ( V , V ) } \left|\alpha\right|=\{V\mid\alpha(V,V)\} . 2. A complexity description a : α ​ ⊳ ​ P ∼ P ∈ ω a{:}\alpha\rhd P\sim P\in\omega induces a measure m a : α . P : | α | → ℕ m_{a{:}\alpha.P}:\left|\alpha\right|\to\mathbb{N} , defined as m a : α . P ( V ) = p m_{a{:}\alpha.P}(V)=p , where [ V / a ] ​ P ⇓ p ¯ [V/a]P\Downarrow\bar{p} . 3. Given a : α ​ ⊳ ​ P ∼ P ∈ ω a{:}\alpha\rhd P\sim P\in\omega and V 1 , V 2 : | α | V_{1},V_{2}:\left|\alpha\right| , write V 1 ⋖ a : α . P V 2 V_{1}\lessdot_{a{:}\alpha.P}V_{2} when m a : α . P V 1 < m a : α . P V 2 m_{a{:}\alpha.P}V_{1}<m_{a{:}\alpha.P}V_{2} . Lemma 6.22 . Given a set A A and a function f : A → ℕ f:A\to\mathbb{N} , ( A , ≺ ) (A,\prec) is well-founded, where a ≺ b ⇔ f ⁡ ( a ) < f ⁡ ( b ) a\prec b\iff f(a)<f(b) . Proof. Suppose S ⊆ A S\subseteq A is inductive: ∀ x ∈ A , ( ∀ y ∈ A , y ≺ x ⟹ y ∈ S ) ⟹ x ∈ S \forall x\in A,(\forall y\in A,y\prec x\implies y\in S)\implies x\in S . It suffices to show S = A S=A , and hence it suffices to show A ⊆ S A\subseteq S . Suppose x ∈ A x\in A . By assumption, it suffices to show ∀ y ∈ A , y ≺ x ⟹ y ∈ S ⇔ ∀ y ∈ A , f ⁡ ( y ) < f ⁡ ( x ) ⟹ y ∈ S \forall y\in A,y\prec x\implies y\in S\iff\forall y\in A,f(y)<f(x)\implies y\in S Proceed by induction on f ⁡ ( x ) f(x) . If f ⁡ ( x ) = 0 f(x)=0 , then the result holds vacuously. Otherwise, let f ⁡ ( x ) = m f(x)=m , and suppose y ∈ A y\in A and f ⁡ ( y ) < m f(y)<m . We need to show y ∈ S y\in S . By the assumption that S S inductive, it suffices to show ∀ z ∈ A , f ⁡ ( z ) < f ⁡ ( y ) ⟹ z ∈ S \forall z\in A,f(z)<f(y)\implies z\in S which holds by induction. ∎ Corollary 6.22.1 . Given a : α ​ ⊳ ​ P ∼ P ∈ ω a{:}\alpha\rhd P\sim P\in\omega , ( | α | , ⋖ a : α . P ) (\left|\alpha\right|,\lessdot_{a{:}\alpha.P}) is a well-founded set. As foreshadowed in the introduction, the proof for the introduction rule proceeds by induction on the well-founded ordering on the inputs to the recursive calls. We conjecture that that this rule is sufficient for verifying all first-order functions. Curried functions and closures present problems in some situations because the first argument does not necessarily decrease in a partial function application. One possible solution to this would be to define an ordering on the resulting closure and demand that partial applications induce a “smaller” closure in an appropriate sense. We leave this for future explorations. Lemma 6.23 (Funtime introduction) . If 1. Now, we extend the operational semantics with additional primitive forms of computation that are assigned unit cost. This is similar to the instruction set of physical machines and represent operations that are considered to be unit cost with the justification that they are usually implemented efficiently in practice. Without these primitives, addition and multiplication defined internally would have linear and super-linear complexities, which seemingly goes against common intuition. The crux of the issue is that these operations have essentially constant time complexity, under the assumption that the inputs fit within the word size of a given machine. Hence, we index the operational semantics with a parameter w w representing the word size and introduce primitives that operate on word-sized inputs. Transition becomes a family of relations _ ↦ w _ : 𝒫 ( 𝖤𝗑𝗉 × 𝖤𝗑𝗉 ) \_\mapsto_{w}\_:\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Exp}\times\mathsf{Exp}) over the parameter w : ℕ w:\mathbb{N} , the maximum value of a number that fits in the word size. There are new syntactic forms f † ⁡ M \mathop{f^{\dagger}}M and M f † N M\mathbin{f^{\dagger}}N representing unary and binary arithmetic operators on natural numbers, respectively. They have the following associated transition steps: E ↦ E ′ f † ⁡ E ↦ f † ⁡ E ′ ​ m < w f † ⁡ m ¯ ↦ f ⁡ ( m ) ¯ ​ E 1 ↦ E 1 ′ E 1 f † E 2 ↦ E 1 ′ f † E 1 ​ V ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 E 2 ↦ E 2 ′ E 1 f † E 2 ↦ E 1 f † E 2 ′ ​ m , n < w m ¯ f † n ¯ ↦ f ⁡ ( m , n ) ¯ \frac{E\mapsto E^{\prime}}{\mathop{f^{\dagger}}E\mapsto\mathop{f^{\dagger}}E^{\prime}}\par\frac{m<w}{\mathop{f^{\dagger}}\bar{m}\mapsto\overline{f(m)}}\par\frac{E_{1}\mapsto E_{1}^{\prime}}{E_{1}\mathbin{f^{\dagger}}E_{2}\mapsto E_{1}^{\prime}\mathbin{f^{\dagger}}E_{1}}\par\frac{V\;\mathsf{val}\quad E_{2}\mapsto E_{2}^{\prime}}{E_{1}\mathbin{f^{\dagger}}E_{2}\mapsto E_{1}\mathbin{f^{\dagger}}E_{2}^{\prime}}\par\frac{m,n<w}{\bar{m}\mathbin{f^{\dagger}}\bar{n}\mapsto\overline{f(m,n)}} In the beta rules, inputs to the operator are required to fit in one word. What is “reasonably” primitive depends on the particular machine one has in mind, but we will postulate that f f can be addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, and modulus. Note that while we already have a foreign function interface via f ^ ⁡ ( M ) \mathop{\hat{f}}(M) and ( M f ^ N ) (M\mathbin{\widehat{f}}N) , these are intended to be used in the cost expressions and not programs. As such, the the foreign function interface has no restriction on the inputs to the operators, and when subjected to complexity verification, invocation of the interface should be thought of as functions calls to an “oracle” that can compute any function in constant time. This is useful when one is interested in a more abstract measure of cost, such as the number of recursive calls a function makes as opposed to the actual running time of said function. There is a new associated elimination rule for primitive computations on natural numbers: Lemma 7.1 (Primitive elimination) . If 1. If V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 𝚗𝚊𝚝 V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}\mathtt{nat} and P ∈ V < ^ w ¯ P\in V\mathrel{\widehat{<}}\bar{w} , then f † ⁡ V ≐ f † ⁡ V ′ ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ⁡ [ 1 ¯ ] \mathop{f^{\dagger}}V\doteq\mathop{f^{\dagger}}V^{\prime}\in\mathtt{nat}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\bar{1}]}} . 2. If V ≐ V ′ ∈ 0 𝚗𝚊𝚝 V\doteq V^{\prime}\in_{0}\mathtt{nat} , U ≐ U ′ ∈ 0 𝚗𝚊𝚝 U\doteq U^{\prime}\in_{0}\mathtt{nat} , P ∈ V < ^ w ¯ P\in V\mathrel{\widehat{<}}\bar{w} , Q ∈ U < ^ w ¯ Q\in U\mathrel{\widehat{<}}\bar{w} , then V f † U ≐ V f † U ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ⁡ [ 1 ¯ ] V\mathbin{f^{\dagger}}U\doteq V\mathbin{f^{\dagger}}U\in\mathtt{nat}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[\bar{1}]}} . Proof. Similar to proof of Lemma 6.8 . ∎ 8 Example: greatest common divisor We are now equipped to state and analyze the computational complexity for a general class of programs. We focus on analyzing the complexity of Euclid’s algorithm for computing the gcd of two natural numbers. This is a prototypical example of an algorithm which is not structurally recursive, and we demonstrate that the semantic framework of 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} is capable of handling such recursive constructions. Remark . For presentation purposes, we derive a basic bound and acknowledge that it’s not the tightest characterization. It is certainly possible to carry out a more detailed analysis in 𝐂𝐀𝐓𝐓 \mathbf{CATT} . Let the ternary predicate gcdProp : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℕ × ℕ × ℕ ) \textit{gcdProp}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}) be defined as gcd ​ ( d , m , n ) \textit{gcd}(d,m,n) if d d is the gcd of m m and n n . This is then internalized as g ​ c ​ d ​ P ​ r ​ o ​ p ^ ⁡ ( D , M , N ) \mathop{\widehat{gcdProp}}(D,M,N) for D , M , N ∈ 𝚗𝚊𝚝 D,M,N\in\mathtt{nat} . It is possible to define this predicate internally, but it is convenient to delegate this routine verification to the metatheory. Let the gcd algorithm be defined as follows: gcd ≜ \displaystyle\textit{gcd}\triangleq\, 𝚏𝚞𝚗 ​ a ​ f ​ 𝚒𝚜 \displaystyle\mathtt{fun}\,a\,f\,\mathtt{is}\, 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ x = a ⋅ 𝟷 ​ 𝚒𝚗 \displaystyle\mathtt{let}\;x=a\cdot\mathtt{1}\;\mathtt{in}\; 𝚕𝚎𝚝 ​ y = a ⋅ 𝟸 ​ 𝚒𝚗 \displaystyle\mathtt{let}\;y=a\cdot\mathtt{2}\;\mathtt{in}\; 𝚒𝚏𝚣 ( x ) { y ; x ′ . 𝚕𝚎𝚝 z = y % † 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ( x ′ ) 𝚒𝚗 f ⟨ z , 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ( x ′ ) ⟩ } \displaystyle\mathtt{ifz}(x)\{y;x^{\prime}.\mathtt{let}\;z=y\mathbin{\%^{\dagger}}{\mathtt{suc}}(x^{\prime})\;\mathtt{in}\;f\;\langle z,{\mathtt{suc}}(x^{\prime})\rangle\} Let A \displaystyle A = { x : 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ∣ x < ^ w ¯ } × { y : 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ∣ y < ^ w ¯ } \displaystyle=\{x{:}\mathtt{nat}\mid x\mathrel{\widehat{<}}\bar{w}\}\times\{y{:}\mathtt{nat}\mid y\mathrel{\widehat{<}}\bar{w}\} B \displaystyle B = ⟨ a ⋅ 𝟷 / x ⟩ ⟨ a ⋅ 𝟸 / y ⟩ { d : 𝚗𝚊𝚝 ∣ g ​ c ​ d ​ P ​ r ​ o ​ p ^ ( d , x , y ) } \displaystyle=\left<a\cdot\mathtt{1}/x\right>\left<a\cdot\mathtt{2}/y\right>\{d{:}\mathtt{nat}\mid\mathop{\widehat{gcdProp}}(d,x,y)\} P \displaystyle P = 1 ¯ + ^ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ( ⟨ a ⋅ 𝟷 / x ⟩ 1 ¯ + ^ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ( ⟨ a ⋅ 𝟸 / y ⟩ 𝚒𝚏𝚣 ( x ) { 1 ¯ ; x ′ . 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ( 1 ¯ + ^ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ( 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ( 8 ¯ × ^ 𝚜𝚞𝚌 ( x ′ ) ) ) ) } ) ) \displaystyle=\bar{1}\hat{+}{\mathtt{suc}}(\left<a\cdot\mathtt{1}/x\right>\bar{1}\hat{+}{\mathtt{suc}}(\left<a\cdot\mathtt{2}/y\right>\mathtt{ifz}(x)\{\bar{1};x^{\prime}.{\mathtt{suc}}(\bar{1}\hat{+}{\mathtt{suc}}({\mathtt{suc}}(\bar{8}\hat{\times}{\mathtt{suc}}(x^{\prime}))))\})) We will verify the following specification: gcd ∈ 0 a : A → B [ P ] \textit{gcd}\in_{0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a{:}A\to B}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{[P]}} Remark . Note that we deliberately program in a fashion commonly referred to as monadic form (or sometimes A-normal form) even though the operational semantics places no restriction on the presentation of programs. This is because the proof theory was arranged to facilitate verification of programs of this form. To verify programs written in other styles, different collections of lemmas would be appropriate. To extend the theory to account for parallel programs, we arrange the operational semantics so that each transition step corresponds to one unit of cost in the span of the computation. The idea is that sub-terms of a computation that are independent of each other transition simultaneously whenever possible. Intuitively, the resulting cost measures the length of the longest sequence of dependencies in the computation. Figure 6 contains selected rules of the parallel operational semantics of 𝐏𝐋 \mathbf{PL} . The omitted rules remain the same from the sequential operational semantics. The unary sequential composition is generalized to the binary sequencing operator 𝚙𝚊𝚛 ( E 1 ; E 2 ; a . b . E 3 ) \mathtt{par}(E_{1};E_{2};a.b.E_{3}) , which transitions E 1 E_{1} and E 2 E_{2} simultaneously and binds the results to a a and b b for use in E 3 E_{3} . Binary sequencing can be taken as a primitive. Here we define it in terms of unary sequencing: 𝚙𝚊𝚛 ( E 1 ; E 2 ; a . b . E 3 ) ≜ 𝚕𝚎𝚝 c = ⟨ E 1 , E 2 ⟩ 𝚒𝚗 𝚕𝚎𝚝 a = c ⋅ 𝟷 𝚒𝚗 𝚕𝚎𝚝 b = c ⋅ 𝟸 𝚒𝚗 E 3 \mathtt{par}(E_{1};E_{2};a.b.E_{3})\triangleq\mathtt{let}\;c=\langle E_{1},E_{2}\rangle\;\mathtt{in}\;\mathtt{let}\;a=c\cdot\mathtt{1}\;\mathtt{in}\;\mathtt{let}\;b=c\cdot\mathtt{2}\;\mathtt{in}\;E_{3} As before, there is the notion of evaluation: 1. M ⇓ c V M\Downarrow^{c}V when M ↦ c V M\mapsto^{c}V and V ​ 𝗏𝖺𝗅 V\;\mathsf{val} . 2. M ⇓ V M\Downarrow V when there is a c c s.t. M ⇓ c V M\Downarrow^{c}V . However, c c now represents the span, i.e., the parallel complexity of the computation M M . The construction of the type system τ ω \tau_{\omega} and the associated proof theory is unaffected by the change of the operational semantics. There is an associated derived binary sequencing rule: Lemma 9.1 (Binary sequence) . If 1. M 1 ≐ M 1 ′ ∈ A 1 ​ [ P 1 ] M_{1}\doteq M_{1}^{\prime}\in A_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P_{1}]}} 2. M 2 ≐ M 2 ′ ∈ A 2 ​ [ P 2 ] M_{2}\doteq M_{2}^{\prime}\in A_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[P_{2}]}} 3. a 1 : A 1 , a 2 : A 2 ≫ N ≐ N ′ ∈ B ⁡ [ Q ] a_{1}{:}A_{1},a_{2}{:}A_{2}\gg N\doteq N^{\prime}\in B{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\;[Q]}} then 𝚙𝚊𝚛 ( M 1 ; M 2 ; a 1 . a 2 .
